Non-compliance with POSH provisions: fine up to ₹50,000; repeat →
double fine + licence cancellation / deregistration

₹50,000 — specific number
often tested

✔ KEY FACT
POSH remains standalone — not subsumed in any Labour Code: The four Labour Codes (Wages, IR, SS,
OSH) do NOT subsume POSH. This is frequently a distractor in MCQs. POSH 2013 continues as a separate statute
alongside the Labour Codes. Similarly, the Maternity Benefit Act 1961 remains partly operative — maternity
leave provisions are in SS Code but crèche provisions remain in MBA.

MNEMONIC
POSH triggers: 10+ → IC (woman chair) · <10 → LC (District Officer) · Complaint: 3+3 months ·
Inquiry: 90 days · Interim leave: 3 months · Employer acts: 60 days · Fine: ₹50,000
QUICK RECALL
IC mandatory when: 10+ employees
IC Chairperson: Woman employee at senior level
External member: Mandatory 1 from NGO
Half of IC: Must be women
LC constituted by: District Officer
Complaint time limit: 3 months + 3 months extension
Inquiry completion: 90 days from complaint
Interim leave: Up to 3 months (additional)
Conciliation trigger: Only aggrieved woman's written request
Employer fine: ₹50,000 for non-compliance
Appeal time: 90 days from employer action

Cases
The EO/AO 2025 exam (Nov 2025) asked 15 Labour Law questions covering provisions that APFC

A. IDA 1947 — Strike / Lock-out Notice (Section 22) — BOTH 6-weeks AND 14-days
⚠ EXAM ALERT

EPFO APFC Complete Study Notes · Page 78 · Verified April 2026

Section 22 IDA 1947 — the COMPLETE rule for public utility services:
No person employed in a public utility service shall go on strike (or lock-out) unless:
(a) Notice of strike given to the employer within 6 weeks before striking (i.e., notice given not more than 6
weeks in advance);
(b) Strike does NOT commence within 14 days of giving such notice (14-day cooling-off period).
TRAP 1: Current notes say '14 days notice' — that is only the COOLING period. The full window is 6 weeks
(notice must be within 6 weeks before the strike date). EO/AO 2025 Q46 tested '6 weeks notice' — answer =
applies to BOTH workmen before strike AND employers before lock-out in PU service. NOT applicable to non-PU
industries.
Rule

Public Utility Service

Non-PU Industry

Notice required before strike

6 weeks advance + 14-day cooling off

No statutory advance notice required

Lock-out notice

Same — 6 weeks + 14 days

No statutory advance notice

Under IR Code 2020

60 days notice for ALL industries

Same 60-day rule applies universally

Who must give notice

Both workmen (before strike) AND employer
(before lock-out) in PU service

N/A

EC Act Numerical Drill (EO/AO 2025 Q43):
Worker age 48; mine fatal accident; monthly wage ₹12,000; Relevant Factor = 159.80.
Compensation (death) = 50% × monthly wage × Relevant Factor
= 50% × 12,000 × 159.80 = 6,000 × 159.80 = ₹9,58,800
Note: Relevant Factor depends on age — taken from Schedule IV of ECA 1923. For age 48, various sources give
159.80 to 174.63 depending on notification date. Use the factor given in the question. Formula is what
matters: 50% × wage × factor.

E. Payment of Gratuity Act — Death Needs ZERO Minimum Service
⚠ EXAM ALERT
EO/AO 2025 Q47 — all three scenarios tested:
1. Resign after 6 years → ✓ Gratuity payable (resignation requires 5 years minimum)
2. Dies after 2 years → ✓ Gratuity payable (DEATH / DISABLEMENT = NO minimum service requirement — even
1 month of service qualifies)
3. Retires after exactly 5 years → ✓ Gratuity payable (5 years = the threshold)
Answer = B (1, 2 and 3 — all three eligible)
KEY RULE: Only in case of Death or Disablement is the 5-year minimum waived. For all other exits (resignation,
retrenchment, retirement), 5 continuous years of service is the mandatory minimum.

Chapter 22E — APFC Dec 2025 Labour Law Drill (Q81–Q95)
Every labour law question from the APFC Dec 2025 (CRT-IX) paper. These 15 questions define exactly
what gets tested. Study each answer and its trap — the same patterns will repeat in future APFC exams.
Q

Topic / Key Fact

Answer / Trap

Q81

EPF Act — which scheme does NOT
currently exist?

Employees' Family Pension Scheme, 1971 — it was replaced by
EPS 1995. Currently existing: EPF Scheme 1952, EDLI 1976, EPS
1995. Trap: FPS 1971 sounds like a pension scheme.

Q82

EPF Act exemption — co-operative
societies

EPF Act does NOT apply to co-operative societies employing fewer
than 50 persons AND working WITHOUT power. Both conditions
must be met. >50 persons = covered even without power.

Q83

ESI contributions — numerical
(₹20,000 wage)

Employer = 3.25% × ₹20,000 = ₹650. Employee = 0.75% ×
₹20,000 = ₹150. Total = ₹800 (4% combined). Old rates
(pre-2019): 4.75%+1.75%.

Q84

ESI Act — which is NOT a named
benefit?

'Health benefit' is NOT a named benefit under ESI Act. Correct
term = Medical benefit. Named ESI benefits: Sickness, Maternity,
Disablement, Dependants', Medical benefit.

Q85

Payment of Wages Act 1936 —
statements

TRUE: wages can be paid in current coins. TRUE: wage period can
be fixed for weekly payment. FALSE: '7th day for establishments of
1000+ workers' — correct rule is 7th day for ≤1000 workers;
10th day for >1000 workers.

Q86

Payment of Wages Act — permissible
deductions

All three are allowed: (1) deductions for amenities/services, (2)
recovery of advances, (3) payments to co-operative societies. NOT
allowed: unauthorized deductions beyond 75% of wages.

Q87

EC Act 1923 — one eye lost, other
normal

= Permanent PARTIAL disablement (Schedule I Part II). NOT
total. Both eyes lost = total. One eye = 40% of PTD amount. PTD
formula: 60% × monthly wage × Relevant Factor.

Q88

EC Act — fatal accident calculation
(age 48, ₹12,000/month)

Formula: 50% × monthly wage × Relevant Factor. Age 48 factor =
159.80. Calculation: 50% × 12,000 × 159.80 = 6,000 × 159.80 =
₹9,58,800.

Q89

IDA — triple test for 'industry' —
landmark case

Bangalore Water Supply & Sewerage Board v. R. Rajappa
AIR 1978 SC 548 (7-judge bench) gave the definitive triple test.
Three elements: systematic activity + employer-employee
relationship + production/distribution of goods/services.

Q90

IDA First Schedule — which is NOT a
Public Utility Service?

Pyrites Mining is NOT a PU service. Tea Plantation, Chemical
Fertilizer Industry, and Alumina/Aluminium manufacture ARE in IDA
First Schedule. Pyrites = iron sulfide mineral — not listed.

Q91

IDA Sec 22 — 6-week notice applies to
whom?

Applies to: (1) workmen in PUS before strike AND (2)
employers in PUS before lockout. Does NOT apply to workmen
in ANY service — only Public Utility Services. Answer = 1 and 2 only.

Q92

Gratuity payable in which situations?
(6 yr resignation, 2 yr death, 5 yr
retirement)

ALL THREE qualify. (1) 6-year resignation: 6 ≥ 5 minimum ✓. (2)
2-year death: no minimum for death/disablement ✓. (3) 5-year
retirement: 5 = exactly the minimum ✓.

Q93

TU Act — which is NOT a right under
Chapter III?

NOT a right: committing a tortious act by an agent of TU
without knowledge of the executive. Rights include: political
fund constitution, 17-year-old membership, changing employment
contract in contemplation of trade dispute.

Q94

Maternity Benefit — when can
government servant claim 26 weeks?

Never — government servants are EXCLUDED from MBA 1961
(they use CCS Leave Rules). Within MBA: commissioning mother
with no surviving child → 12 weeks (not 26). 26 weeks only for
biological mother with up to 2 surviving children.

Q95

Nursing breaks under Maternity
Benefit Act 1961

Two breaks IN ADDITION TO the interval for rest. NOT within the
rest interval — they are extra. Continues until child is 15 months
old.

PART VII

Indian Economy
Mental Model First · Then the Numbers

Chapter 24 — How to Think About the Indian Economy
Before any formula, build the mental model. The Indian economy is a giant household with three
managers, dealing with one neighbour. If you remember this picture, every chapter that follows will plug
in naturally — and you will retain it because each fact has a home in the picture.

24.1 The Picture — One Household, Three Managers, One Neighbour
Imagine a large joint family (= the Indian economy):
The Household — 1.4 billion citizens who produce goods and services (this is what GDP measures) and
earn income from that production. Everything else exists to manage this household.
Manager #1 — RBI (the Money Manager). Controls how much money is in circulation. If too much
money chases too few goods → inflation rises → RBI tightens the tap (hikes Repo). If money is scarce and
growth stalls → RBI opens the tap (cuts Repo). Tools: Repo, CRR, SLR, SDF, MSF, OMO. This is Chapter
27.
Manager #2 — Government (the Budget Manager). Collects taxes (direct + indirect) and spends on
schemes, salaries, infrastructure, defence. When spending > income, there's a deficit — financed by
borrowing. This is Chapter 28.
Manager #3 — Planners (the Strategy Manager). Sets long-term direction: Five-Year Plans
(1951-2017, Planning Commission) → NITI Aayog (2015 onward) — now a think-tank, not a money
allocator. This is Chapter 29.
The Neighbour — The World. India trades with it. Imports, exports, FDI, exchange rate, BoP — this is
the External Sector. Schemes like ELI, MUDRA, MSME policy try to make the household stronger so it can
compete. Chapter 30 covers schemes.

24.2 The 4-Pillar Map — Where Every Fact Lives
Pillar

Question it answers

Chapter

do we measure it?

24.3 The Master Mnemonic — 'MFP+S'
✔ KEY FACT
Monetary · Fiscal · Planning · Schemes → MFP+S
Before reading ANY economy paragraph, ask: 'Which pillar is this?' If you can't place it in M, F, P, or S — you are
probably in the National Income primer (Pillar 0). With this filter alone, 70% of economy questions become
easier.

EPFO APFC Complete Study Notes · Page 84 · Verified April 2026

24.4 Why APFC Cares About Economy (the connection)
EPFO's mandate is workforce financial security. To understand your future job, you need to
understand: (a) how the economy generates jobs (Pillar 0 + Pillar 3 schemes like ELI), (b) how inflation
erodes the value of pension payouts (Pillar 1 → MPC's 4% target keeps EPF real returns positive), (c) how
Govt fiscal capacity affects subsidy schemes for unorganised workers (Pillar 2 → CESS on aggregators
under SS Code), and (d) how MSMEs employ 11 crore workers but only 15% are EPF-covered — your
enforcement target (Pillar 3).

24.5 Self-Check Before You Move On
✔ KEY FACT
Cover the right side. Answer aloud. Move on only when all 5 are clean.
1. Name the 4 pillars and what each answers → MFP+S; Money/Tax/Plan/Schemes
2. Which pillar handles inflation? → Pillar 1 (Monetary, RBI)
3. Which pillar handles Budget? → Pillar 2 (Fiscal)
4. Planning Commission replaced by ___? → NITI Aayog (2015)
5. Why does an APFC need to know this? → Jobs, pension real-value, MSME enforcement

EPFO APFC Complete Study Notes · Page 85 · Verified April 2026

Chapter 25 — National Income — How Big Is the Economy?
The Question: If your boss asks 'how much did India produce this year?', what number do you give? The
honest answer: it depends on what you mean. Do you mean the value of stuff made INSIDE India's borders
(GDP) or stuff made BY Indians anywhere (GNP)? Should you count the depreciation of factories
(NDP/NNP)? Should you subtract taxes (Factor cost vs Market price)? Each subtle difference gives a
different number — and the exam loves these differences.

25.0 The Story — Why So Many Aggregates?
Think of national income like measuring a person's wealth. GDP is the gross monthly salary printed on the
payslip. GNP adds any income they earn from abroad (NFIA) but subtracts what foreigners earn from
India. NNP subtracts wear-and-tear on assets (your car depreciating). NI at factor cost strips out indirect
taxes. PI arrives at the family's actual income (subtracting corporate tax, adding transfers). DPI is the
take-home after personal income tax — what you can actually spend. The exam will throw any of these at
you and ask you to walk one step up or down the chain.
✔ KEY FACT
Memory Hook — 'GGN-NPD' (Going Goes Net Net Personal Disposable):
GDP → GNP (add foreign income) → NNP (subtract depreciation) → NI (subtract indirect taxes, at factor cost)
→ PI (subtract corporate tax, add transfers) → DPI (subtract direct tax).
Each arrow is a SUBTRACTION or ADJUSTMENT. The chain goes from gross-est (GDP) to most personal (DPI =
what you actually spend).

25.1 The Aggregate Chain
GDP (Gross Domestic Product)
Value of goods & services within borders

GNP = GDP + NFIA
Income from abroad accounted

NNP = GNP − Depreciation
Net of capital consumption

NI

= NNP at factor cost
Exclude net indirect taxes

PI

= NI − Corporate tax + Transfers
Individual income accounting

DPI = PI − Direct tax
Disposable — the take-home

Figure 25.1 — National-income chain

25.2 Each Aggregate — formula & meaning
Aggregate
GDP

Formula
C + I + G + (X − M)

What it measures
Final output produced within country (expenditure method)

FORMULA / KEY POINTS
GDP (Expenditure method) = C + I + G + (X − M)
C = Private consumption expenditure
I = Gross investment (private + public capital formation)
G = Government consumption expenditure
X − M = Net exports (exports minus imports)

✔ KEY FACT
GDP at constant prices uses base year 2011-12 in India. The NSO is now moving to a new base year
2022-23 for the CPI and WPI series — the GDP base change is under consultation as of April 2026.

MNEMONIC

→ NI → PI → DPI
QUICK RECALL
Domestic vs National: GDP = domestic; GNP = national (residents)
NFIA: Net Factor Income from Abroad
Base year (GDP): 2011-12 (new series under discussion)
Factor vs Market: Factor cost = Market price − Net indirect taxes + Subsidies
Biggest GDP share: Services sector (~54%) — India, FY 2024-25

Chapter 27 — Monetary Policy — RBI as the Money Manager
The Story: Imagine the economy as a bathtub. Money flowing IN = the tap; money flowing OUT (back to
RBI) = the drain. Too much water (money) → overflow (inflation). Too little → bathtub goes empty (growth
stalls). The RBI's job is to keep the level just right. The tools below are different ways to control the tap
and drain.

27.0 The Tap & Drain Mental Model
Direction

Tool

What it does (in plain English)

TAP open (more money
out)

Repo cut · OMO buy · CRR cut · SLR
cut

Banks borrow cheaper from RBI → lend cheaper to
public → more loans → demand ↑ → growth ↑ (but
inflation risk ↑)

DRAIN open (less money
out)

Repo hike · OMO sell · CRR hike ·
SLR hike · SDF

Banks borrow costlier → lending costlier → loans ↓ →
demand ↓ → inflation ↓ (but growth slows)

✔ KEY FACT
Mental anchor: Whenever you see 'RBI hiked Repo' → think drain open, fighting inflation, slowing economy.
Whenever you see 'RBI cut Repo' → think tap open, boosting demand, accepting some inflation risk. MPC's 4%
± 2% target means: inflation between 2% and 6% = OK; outside the band = failure → write to Government
explaining why.

✔ KEY FACT
STUDY FILTER — Monetary Policy & RBI | Tier 1 (2-3 marks)
MEMORIZE: MPC = 6 members (3 RBI + 3 external) · Inflation target = 4% ± 2% (CPI Headline) · Bi-monthly
meetings · Quorum = 4 of 6 · Governor casts tie-breaker · Sec 45ZB of RBI Act = legal basis (2016 amendment)
· SDF replaced Reverse Repo (Apr 2022) · Tools: Repo · CRR · SLR · SDF · MSF · OMO · RBI Governor 2026
= Sanjay Malhotra (from 11 Dec 2024)
SKIP: Specific Repo rate history year-by-year (just know current is ~6.5%) · Detailed MPC voting records · Bank
Rate (now aligned with MSF) · Pre-2016 monetary policy framework history
TRAP: Inflation TARGET (4%) ≠ Band (2-6%). 'Reverse Repo' is now obsolete (SDF since Apr 2022). RBI is NOT
constitutional — established under RBI Act 1934, nationalised 1 Jan 1949.

Who decides? The Monetary Policy Committee (MPC) — 6 members: 3 from RBI (Governor + DG +
Executive Director) + 3 external experts nominated by Central Government. Meets bi-monthly. Majority
vote decides; Governor casts the tie-breaker. Quorum = 4 of 6. Legal basis: Section 45ZB of RBI Act
(added by 2016 amendment).

27.1 MPC Structure

(Chairperson)

27.2 Key Tools of the RBI
Tool

What it is

Mechanism

Repo Rate

Rate at which RBI lends to banks (short-term)

Hike → costlier borrowing → demand ↓

Reverse
Repo

Rate at which banks park surplus with RBI

Now subsumed by SDF

Standing Deposit Facility (Apr 2022)

Floor of liquidity corridor; replaces Reverse Repo

Marginal Standing Facility rate

Emergency overnight window; Repo + 0.25%

CRR

Cash Reserve Ratio (% of NDTL kept as cash
with RBI)

No interest paid; direct liquidity absorption

SLR

Statutory Liquidity Ratio (% in govt securities)

Enhances financial stability

OMO

Open Market Operations

Buy/sell of government bonds by RBI

Bank Rate

Long-term lending rate (historical)

Aligned with MSF currently

27.3 Transmission of a Rate Hike

EPFO APFC Complete Study Notes · Page 91 · Verified April 2026

Repo rate hike by MPC
Signals tightening

Bank borrowing costs rise
Funded via more expensive money

Loan interest rates to public rise
MCLR / RLLR re-pricing

Consumer & business demand falls
Big-ticket items deferred

Headline inflation begins to fall
Usually 2-4 quarters lag

Figure 27.2 — Transmission channel of a repo hike

FORMULA / KEY POINTS
MPC mandate: CPI headline inflation = 4% ± 2% (band 2-6%)
Legal basis: RBI Act amendment 2016; Finance Act 2016 Section 45ZB
Quorum: 4 of 6 members; decisions by majority; Governor casts the tie-breaker

⚠ EXAM ALERT
SDF (Standing Deposit Facility) was introduced in April 2022 and effectively replaced Reverse Repo as the
liquidity corridor floor. Answering "Reverse Repo" as the current floor is a common trap.

Chapter 28 — Fiscal Policy — Government as the Budget Manager
The Story: Imagine your household. You have income (salary, gifts, side hustle) and expenses (rent,
groceries, EMI, school fees). If you spend more than you earn, you borrow — that's a deficit. The
Government of India is exactly the same, just bigger. Fiscal policy = how Govt manages this household
budget.

28.0 The Household Mental Model
Household concept

Government
equivalent

Examples

Income from salary, rent

Revenue Receipts

Tax (GST, IT, corporate); non-tax (RBI dividends, PSU
profit)

Selling old car, getting loan
back

Capital Receipts

Disinvestment, loan repayments, borrowings

Rent, groceries, EMI interest

Revenue Expenditure

Salaries, pensions, subsidies, INTEREST on debt

Buying a house, building

Capital Expenditure

Highways, defence equipment, Chandrayaan, LOAN GIVEN

Borrowing when short on cash

Fiscal Deficit

Total spending − total receipts (excluding borrowings)

✔ KEY FACT
The Three Deficits Memory Trick (use household analogy):
Fiscal Deficit (FD) = TOTAL borrowing need = 'My salary won't cover everything; how much must I borrow?'
Revenue Deficit (RD) = day-to-day shortfall = 'My salary is short for even my groceries and rent.'
Primary Deficit (PD) = FD − interest = 'Forget old debt for a moment; do I have a NEW borrowing need?'
If PD = 0, government is borrowing ONLY to pay interest on old debt (no fresh expansion). This is the most
important deficit conceptually — it shows whether the government is still structurally overspending.

Why three deficits and not one? Because each tells a different story. FD tells you total borrowing
burden. RD tells you whether you're borrowing for recurring stuff (bad — eating into future capacity) or
asset creation (good). PD tells you if the situation is improving (PD shrinking) or whether debt is
compounding on itself.
The Calendar: Budget presented on 1 February by the Finance Minister → debated in Parliament →
voted by 31 March → operative from 1 April (start of financial year). FRBM Act 2003 sets the targets:
original target was 3% fiscal deficit. Actual: above target for years. Budget 2026-27 pegged FD at 4.3%
(down from 4.4% target in 2025-26). The 'glide path' to 3% continues.
✔ KEY FACT

EPFO APFC Complete Study Notes · Page 94 · Verified April 2026

STUDY FILTER — Fiscal Policy & Budget | Tier 1 (1-2 marks)
MEMORIZE: FD = Total Exp − Total Receipts (ex borrowings) · RD = Rev Exp − Rev Receipts · PD = FD
− Interest Payments · FRBM Act 2003 — original target 3% GDP · FY 2026-27 target = 4.3% · Budget = 1
Feb (operative 1 April) · Capital Exp = asset-creating (highways, defence); Revenue Exp = recurring (salaries,
interest, subsidies) · Loans GIVEN by Govt = Capital Exp; Interest RECEIVED = Revenue Receipt; Principal
repayment = Capital Receipt
SKIP: Year-by-year deficit numbers (just know current 4.3%) · FRBM Act amendments details · Specific Article
numbers for Budget (Art 112 = Annual Financial Statement)
TRAP: 'Loan given by Govt' = CAPITAL exp (NOT revenue). 'Interest received' on those loans = REVENUE
receipt. 'Principal repayment received' = CAPITAL receipt. Three deficits = TOTAL borrowing (FD), RECURRING
gap (RD), STRUCTURAL gap excl old debt (PD).

28.2 Capital vs Revenue Expenditure
CAPITAL EXPENDITURE
• Creates assets (roads, rail, schools, defence)
• Non-recurring in nature
• Raises future productive capacity
• Examples: Highways (NHAI); rail lines;
Chandrayaan

REVENUE EXPENDITURE
• Does NOT create assets
• Recurring — salaries, pensions, interest, subsidies
• Needed for day-to-day running
• Examples: Pay Commission; MGNREGS wages;
interest on debt

⚠ EXAM ALERT
A common exam trap: Loans given by the Govt are Capital Expenditure; interest received on those
loans is Revenue Receipt. The repayment of principal of loans by borrowers is treated as a Capital
Receipt.

MNEMONIC
FD = TE − TR · RD = RE − RR · PD = FD − Int

Q1: Fiscal Deficit formula? → Total Exp − Total Receipts (ex borrowings)

Q12: FY 2026-27 target? → 4.3% of GDP
EXAM-EVE CHECKLIST:
□ FD = total borrowing need; RD = recurring shortfall; PD = excl. interest burden
□ Capital Exp creates ASSET; Revenue Exp doesn't
□ Loans given = CAPITAL (memorize this — common trap)

Chapter 29 — Planning Story — From Soviet-Style Plans to a
Think-Tank
The Story (in three acts):
Act I (1950-1991) — The Command Era. Newly independent India looked at war-shattered Europe
rebuilding through plans, and at the USSR's centralised model. PM Nehru set up the Planning
Commission (15 March 1950) via a Cabinet resolution (NOT a constitutional body). The First Five-Year
Plan (1951-56) focused on agriculture; the Second (Mahalanobis model) on heavy industry. The
government decided which factory got steel, which state got a dam.
Act II (1991-2014) — The Mismatch. After 1991 liberalisation, the economy became market-driven. Yet
the Planning Commission kept telling states how much money they could spend (the 'Gosplan' style).
Critics: 'why is a body designed for a socialist economy allocating money in a market economy?' Tension
built up.
Act III (2014-now) — The Pivot. On 13 August 2014, PM Modi announced from the Red Fort that the
Planning Commission would be dissolved. On 1 January 2015, NITI Aayog was born (National Institution
for Transforming India). Critical change: NITI does NOT allocate money. It is a think-tank +
cooperative federalism platform. Money allocation is now done by Finance Commission (Centre-State
sharing) and Finance Ministry (within the Budget). The 12th Five-Year Plan (2012-17) was the last.

29.0 The Pivot in One Table
Aspect

Planning Commission (1950-2014)

NITI Aayog (2015-)

Legal status

Cabinet resolution (NOT constitutional)

Cabinet resolution (NOT constitutional)

Primary role

Resource allocator + plan-maker

all CMs)

✔ KEY FACT
The 3 Numbers to Anchor (forever):
1950 — Planning Commission set up (15 March)
2014 — Planning Commission dissolved (13 August announcement)
2015 — NITI Aayog founded (1 January)
The 1-line trap: Both Planning Commission AND NITI Aayog are/were NOT constitutional bodies. Both
created by Cabinet resolution. If a question says 'NITI Aayog is constitutional' → WRONG. If a question says 'NITI
Aayog allocates money to states' → WRONG (Finance Commission does).

✔ KEY FACT
NITI Aayog ≠ Planning Commission. NITI does NOT allocate funds. Its role: (i) policy advice to Centre &
States; (ii) Cooperative federalism (Governing Council with all CMs); (iii) Competitive federalism (SDG Index,
Aspirational Districts, etc.). Chairperson = PM; Vice-Chairperson is the operational head.

⚠ EXAM ALERT
The Mahalanobis model underpinned the Second Plan (1956-61) and pushed India's heavy-industry strategy.
The Harrod-Domar model underpinned the First Plan. The LPG reforms of 1991 coincided with the 8th Plan.

Chapter 30 — Government Schemes — Sorted by WHO They Help
The Story: Government schemes look overwhelming because they all sound similar (PMJJBY, PMSBY, APY,
PM-SYM... your head spins). The trick is to NOT memorize them as a flat list. Instead, ask 'Who is this
scheme for?' Group by target audience, and 30 schemes shrink to 5 mental buckets.

Ch-30 APFC Lens — Labour Economics & Social Security Framework
Economy questions in APFC paper are almost always filtered through the labour & social security lens.
Pure macro questions are rare; what the paper-setter tests is: 'Does the candidate understand the
economic context in which EPFO operates?' Memorise the numbers below.

✔ KEY FACT
ILO & India milestones: India was a founding member of the ILO (1919). First Indian labour law (Factories Act
1881) predated ILO. India has ratified 47 of 189 ILO Conventions (as of 2024) — including 6 of 8 'fundamental'
conventions; pending are Convention 87 (Freedom of Association) and Convention 98 (Right to Organise &
Collective Bargaining).

Chapter 30 — Economy PYQ Drill — EO/AO 2025 Precision Facts
A. Core Inflation — EXCLUDES Both Food AND Energy
⚠ EXAM ALERT
Core Inflation = Headline CPI − Food − Energy
Core inflation excludes BOTH food prices AND energy prices because these are highly volatile and seasonal
— they mask the underlying demand-side inflationary pressure.
EO/AO 2025 Q99: 'Core inflation includes food prices (1) and energy prices (2)?' → Answer D = Neither 1 nor 2.
Both are excluded.
MPC targets headline CPI (4% ± 2%) but watches core inflation to gauge structural demand pressure. India's
core CPI is published by NSO.

are NOT a criterion. Use the April 2025 figures for the exam.

Chapter 31 — ILO + International Organisations — Drill
Why this matters for APFC: ILO is the parent body of global labour standards — at least 1-2 ILO
questions every paper. International organisations contribute another 2-3 marks. The trick is to
memorise founding year · HQ · current head · India's status for each.
✔ KEY FACT
STUDY FILTER — ILO + International Orgs | Tier 1 (2-3 marks)
MEMORIZE — ILO (HIGH HIT for APFC): Founded 1919 (Treaty of Versailles) · HQ Geneva · 187 members ·
India = founding member · TRIPARTITE structure · Nobel Peace 1969 · DG = Gilbert Houngbo · 8 Core
Conventions; India did NOT ratify Conv 87 + Conv 98
MEMORIZE — Other Orgs (founded year + HQ): UN (1945, NY) · WHO (1948, Geneva) · WTO (1995
replacing GATT, Geneva, by CONSENSUS) · IMF (1945, Washington) · World Bank (1944, Washington, Pres = Ajay
Banga) · G20 (1999/2008, 21 members after AU 2023) · BRICS (2006, now 9 members after 2024 expansion) ·
SAARC (1985, Kathmandu, dormant since 2014)
SKIP: Memorizing all 187 ILO member countries · Every Convention number beyond the 8 core · Detailed
governance structure of each org · Specific budget figures
TRAP: ILO founded 1919, NOT 1945/46. India ratified 6 of 8 core ILO Conventions; the TWO NOT ratified = 87
(Freedom of Association) + 98 (Collective Bargaining). WTO decisions by CONSENSUS (not majority vote).

✔ KEY FACT
The 8 ILO Core Conventions — India's Position:
India has ratified 6 out of 8 core conventions. The TWO not ratified: Conv 87 (Freedom of Association) and
Conv 98 (Collective Bargaining). Reason: India's existing labour laws (TU Act 1926 etc.) ALREADY guarantee
these rights, so India considers formal ratification unnecessary.
Recent ratification (2017): Conv 138 (Min Age) + Conv 182 (Worst Forms of Child Labour). India is one of
the largest contributors to ILO's regular budget.

Period of overdue

⚠ EXAM ALERT
The NPA Threshold = 90 days overdue. A loan becomes NPA when principal or interest is overdue for more
than 90 days. This is the single most-asked banking number. Before 90 days = SMA. After 90 days = NPA.

PART VIII

Indian Polity & Constitution
Mental Model First · The Constitution as a Building

Chapter 30 — How to Think About the Constitution
Before any Article, build the picture. The Indian Constitution is not 470 random Articles — it is a
building with floors. Each floor has a clear job. If you know which floor an Article lives on, you can guess
its purpose even if you have never seen it before. This chapter gives you the floor plan.

30A.2 The 4 Sources of Inspiration — 'BIG-IF' Mnemonic
The drafters were not original thinkers — they were excellent borrowers. Knowing what we borrowed
from where = ~3-5 marks every exam.
Country / Source

What we borrowed (the famous bits)

Britain (UK)

Parliamentary system · Single citizenship · Rule of Law · Cabinet government · Speaker · Writs ·
Bicameral legislature

Ireland

DPSP · Method of Presidential election · Nominated members of Rajya Sabha

Germany (Weimar)

Emergency provisions (suspension of FR during emergency) · Art 352-360

India's own (GoI Act
1935)

Federal structure · Office of Governor · Public Service Commissions · Emergency provisions
framework (~75% of the Constitution comes from this Act)

France

Republic · 'Liberty, Equality, Fraternity' (in Preamble)

USA

Fundamental Rights · Judicial review · Independence of judiciary · Impeachment · Removal of
judges · Preamble structure · Vice-President as Chair of upper house

USSR (Soviet Union)

Fundamental Duties (added by 42nd Am, 1976) · Five-Year Plans concept · 'Socialist' in
Preamble

Canada

Federation with strong Centre · Residuary powers with Centre · Appointment of Governors ·
Advisory jurisdiction of SC

Australia

Concurrent List · Freedom of trade & commerce (Art 301) · Joint sitting of Parliament

South Africa

Procedure for amendment (Art 368) · Election of Rajya Sabha members

Country / Source
Japan

What we borrowed (the famous bits)
Procedure established by law (Art 21)

✔ KEY FACT
Memory Hook — 'BIG-IF':
Britain → Parliament
Ireland → DPSP
Germany → Emergency
India (GoI 1935) → 75% of structure
France → Liberty Equality Fraternity
Common APFC trap: 'Fundamental Duties' — borrowed from USSR (NOT UK, NOT USA). Added by 42nd
Amendment, 1976. Originally 10 duties; 86th Amendment (2002) added the 11th (parent's duty to
provide education to children 6-14).

30A.3 The 3-Layer Worker-Rights Map (Why APFC cares)

Where in
Constitution

Examples

Enforceability

(sword)

Part III (Fundamental
Rights, Art 12-35)

Art 19(1)(c) form unions · Art 23 no forced

hazardous

in courts (Art 32 + Art
226 writs)

(compass)

(engine)

36: Factories

Centre AND States
both can legislate.
Centre prevails on
conflict (Art 254)

✔ KEY FACT
The mental flow whenever you see a labour Article:
Q: Is it in Part III (rights), Part IV (DPSP), or Seventh Schedule (power)?
If Part III → enforceable; can go to court. Art 19(1)(c), 21, 23, 24.
If Part IV → ideals; state 'shall endeavour'. Art 38, 39, 41, 42, 43, 43A, 43B.
If Concurrent List → who has the power to make the law? Centre + State.

30A.4 Self-Check Before Moving On
✔ KEY FACT
Cover the right. Answer aloud. Don't proceed until all 5 are clean.
1. Which Part contains DPSP? → Part IV (Articles 36-51)
2. Which Part contains FR? → Part III (Articles 12-35)
3. Fundamental Duties were borrowed from? → USSR (added by 42nd Am, 1976)
4. Labour is in which List? → Concurrent (List III) — both Centre & State can legislate
5. Art 23 vs Art 24? → 23 = forced labour/trafficking; 24 = child labour <14 in hazardous

EPFO APFC Complete Study Notes · Page 114 · Verified April 2026

Chapter 31 — Labour Articles — The 3-Layer Walkthrough
The Story: When you read a labour Article, your first question should be: 'Is this giving the worker a
SWORD (right) or just a COMPASS (ideal)?' Swords are in Part III. Compasses are in Part IV. Power to
legislate is in the Seventh Schedule. Below, the Articles are sorted by layer so you remember not just the
number but its function.

31.0 The Worker's Life — Articles in Story Order
Stage 1 — Born into the workforce: Art 24 (no work for children under 14 in hazardous occupations —
fixed by 86th Am 2002 making education a right under Art 21A). Art 23 (no forced labour, no trafficking)
— these are rights.
Stage 2 — Looking for work / forming a group: Art 19(1)(c) — right to form associations / unions.
Art 41 (DPSP) — state shall endeavour to secure right to work, education, public assistance in
unemployment / old age / sickness.
Stage 3 — At work: Art 39(d) — equal pay for equal work, both sexes. Art 39(e) — health & strength of
workers protected. Art 42 — humane conditions of work + maternity relief.
Stage 4 — Pay: Art 43 — LIVING wage (above mere minimum), decent standard, leisure, social &
cultural opportunities. This is THE labour-pay Article in DPSP. Memorise: 43 = Living wage.
Stage 5 — Voice and ownership: Art 43A (added by 42nd Am, 1976) — workers' participation in
management. Art 43B (added by 97th Am, 2011) — promotion of cooperatives.
✔ KEY FACT
The 'A and B' Memory Trick:
Plain Article number (39, 41, 42, 43) → ORIGINAL Constitution (1950).
Article number + A or B → AMENDMENT added it later.
43A = 42nd Amendment (1976) — workers in management
43B = 97th Amendment (2011) — cooperatives
21A = 86th Amendment (2002) — right to education
Whenever you see a letter (A, B, C) suffix, think 'amendment added it.'

✔ KEY FACT
STUDY FILTER — Labour-Relevant Articles | Tier 1 (3-4 marks)
MEMORIZE — Article NUMBERS matter here (iconic): FRs: Art 19(1)(c) = form unions · Art 21 =
life/livelihood · Art 23 = no forced labour/trafficking · Art 24 = no child labour <14 hazardous · DPSP: Art 39(d)
= equal pay · Art 41 = right to work · Art 42 = humane conditions + maternity · Art 43 = LIVING wage (most
asked) · Art 43A = workers in management (42nd Am, 1976) · Art 43B = cooperatives (97th Am, 2011) · Labour
is on Concurrent List (Art 254 — Centre prevails)
SKIP: All other FRs/DPSP not labour-relevant beyond a quick scan · Detailed court rulings on each Article ·
Sub-clauses beyond labour relevance · Memorizing FD list (only labour-relevant duties matter — Art 51A)
TRAP: Art 43 = LIVING wage (NOT 'minimum' or 'fair'). Art 23 (forced labour/trafficking) ≠ Art 24 (child labour)
— different scope. Letter suffix (A, B) = added by amendment. 43A = 42nd Am 1976; 43B = 97th Am 2011.

31.2 Concurrent List Entries (Labour)
FORMULA / KEY POINTS
Entry 22 — Trade unions; industrial and labour disputes
Entry 23 — Social security and social insurance; employment and unemployment
Entry 24 — Welfare of labour incl. conditions of work, PF, employer's liability, workmen's
compensation, invalidity and old-age pensions, maternity benefits
Article 254: When Parliament enacts on a Concurrent List matter, any State law inconsistent with the Central law
is void to that extent.

⚠ EXAM ALERT
Since labour is in the Concurrent List (Entries 22-24), both Parliament and State legislatures can make laws.
This is why you see State-specific Shops & Establishments Acts, Factories Rules, Labour Welfare Fund Acts, etc.
— varying by State. On conflict with Central, Central law prevails (Article 254).

✔ KEY FACT
The EPF & MP Act is a CENTRAL Act enacted under Entry 24 of the Concurrent List. States cannot override its
applicability thresholds or contribution rates.

MNEMONIC
FRs: 19(1)(c) · 21 · 23 · 24 enforce | DPSPs: 38 · 39 · 41 · 42 · 43 · 43A guide

Right to form unions: Art 19(1)(c) FR
Child labour ban (<14): Art 24 FR — in hazardous industries
Forced labour ban: Art 23 FR

32.1 The CAG — in greater depth (EPFO audit)
FORMULA / KEY POINTS
Article 148 — Appointment: President of India, under his hand and seal.
Tenure: 6 years OR 65 years of age, whichever earlier.
Removal: Same manner as a Supreme Court judge — address in each House of Parliament, supported by 2/3 of
members present and voting.
Independence: Salary charged on Consolidated Fund (not voted).
EPFO audit: Under Section 5A(6) of the EPF Act 1952, CAG audits the accounts of the Central Board of
Trustees.
Audit type: Statutory audit (public account expenditure also covered under Article 149).

⚠ EXAM ALERT

EPFO APFC Complete Study Notes · Page 118 · Verified April 2026

CAG tenure = 6 years OR 65 years, whichever comes FIRST, not whichever is LATER. Same rule for UPSC
and ECI members. A common distractor in exams.

✔ KEY FACT
CAG is a constitutional body, but EPFO's Central Board of Trustees (CBT) is a statutory body created by the
EPF Act 1952. The CAG's audit of EPFO is a power granted to a constitutional body by a statute — a classic
example of constitutional + statutory interface.

MNEMONIC
6-65 → tenure rule for CAG · UPSC · ECI (6 years or 65 yrs, whichever earlier)

Finance Comm: Art 280; 16th FC from Jan 2024
UPSC: Art 315; conducts APFC exam
ECI: Art 324; conducts national elections

Ch-32 APFC Lens — Constitutional Provisions, Writ Jurisdiction, & Administrative Law
APFC's polity questions are filtered through three specific angles: (a) constitutional labour-welfare
provisions, (b) writ jurisdiction (because APFC orders go on appeal to HC/tribunals), and (c)
administrative-law principles (APFC is a quasi-judicial authority under S.7A). Master these and you
answer almost every polity question.

A. Constitutional labour & welfare provisions (memorise these articles)

Content

APFC/labour relevance

Reservation in EPFO recruitment; non-discrimination in
service conditions.

Constitutional foundation of trade-union rights. Right to
strike is NOT a fundamental right — T.K. Rangarajan v.
Govt. of TN (2003).

Olga Tellis v. BMC (1985) — includes right to

EPFO APFC Complete Study Notes · Page 120 · Verified April 2026

✔ KEY FACT
APFC's quasi-judicial nature: When conducting a S.7A enquiry, APFC is NOT acting in a purely administrative
capacity — he is quasi-judicial. Consequences: must observe natural justice, issue speaking orders, and his
orders are reviewable under Art 226. See Food Corporation of India v. Provident Fund Commissioner (SC
1990) on quasi-judicial nature.

APFC is: Quasi-judicial authority under S.7A
Writ against EPFO: Art 226 to HC (certiorari + mandamus)
Strike as FR?: NO — T.K. Rangarajan 2003
Natural justice: Audi alteram partem + Nemo judex

Ch-32 Patch — RTI Act 2005 & DPDPA 2023 (transparency vs privacy)
EPFO is a public authority under Section 2(h) of the RTI Act 2005 — subject to disclosure obligations. At
the same time, it handles sensitive personal data (Aadhaar, UAN, bank, wages) and is therefore also
subject to the Digital Personal Data Protection Act 2023. These two statutes now pull in opposite
directions — know both.

A. Right to Information Act 2005
Provision

Content

Long title

Act to provide for setting out the practical regime of right to information for citizens; came into
force 12 Oct 2005.

S.2(h) 'Public
authority'

Any authority established by/under Constitution, law or Govt notification — includes EPFO,
EPFAT, CGIT, all labour tribunals.

S.3 Right to
information

Subject to the provisions of this Act, all citizens shall have the right to information.

S.4 Proactive
disclosure

17 categories must be published suo motu by every public authority — includes budget,
functions, duties, records, decision-making process.

S.6 Request

In writing, or electronically. ₹10 fee (BPL exempt); accompanying for photocopies.

S.7 Time limit

Reply within 30 days of request. 48 hours if concerning life/liberty. Response delay = deemed
refusal.

S.8 Exemptions

10 grounds: national security, trade secrets, privacy (unless overriding public interest), cabinet
papers, etc.

S.11 3rd-party info

Notice to third party before disclosure of info supplied by them.

S.19 Appeal

1st appeal to Appellate Authority within 30 days; 2nd appeal to Information Commission
(CIC/SIC) within 90 days.

S.20 Penalty

PIO can be fined ₹250/day (max ₹25,000) for wrongful refusal / delay / misleading info.

CIC

Chief Information Commissioner + max 10 Information Commissioners; tenure 3 years (capped
at 65 yrs) per 2019 Amendment.

EPFO APFC Complete Study Notes · Page 121 · Verified April 2026

B. Digital Personal Data Protection Act 2023 (DPDPA)
Provision

Content

Assent & scope

Assented 11 Aug 2023. Provisions in force in phases. Covers digital personal data of natural
persons (data principals).

Key concepts

Data Principal (individual whose data is processed) · Data Fiduciary (who determines
purposes & means) · Significant Data Fiduciary (SDF — large-volume)

Lawful bases

Consent (primary) OR 'certain legitimate uses' (employment, public interest, medical
emergency, disaster, legal obligation)

Children's data

Under 18 — verifiable parental consent required; targeted advertising, tracking, behavioural
monitoring of children prohibited

Rights of Data
Principal

Access · Correction / Erasure · Grievance redressal · Nominate (for post-death rights)

Data Fiduciary
obligations

Purpose limitation · Data minimisation · Accuracy · Storage limitation · Security safeguards ·
Breach notification to Board

Penalty

Up to ₹250 crore per instance (for significant personal data breaches)

Regulator

Data Protection Board of India (DPB) — adjudicates complaints & penalties

Overrides RTI?

S.44(3) of DPDPA amends RTI S.8(1)(j) — broader privacy exemption (critics say curtails RTI)

✔ KEY FACT
EPFO as Data Fiduciary: EPFO processes Aadhaar, UAN, bank account, biometric (face-auth for Jeevan
Pramaan), wage details — clearly 'personal data'. As a large-volume processor, it may be notified as an SDF,
triggering additional obligations (DPO appointment, data audit, impact assessments).

MNEMONIC
RTI 2005 → transparency · DPDPA 2023 → privacy · CIC vs DPB

RTI response time: 30 days (48 hrs life/liberty)
RTI appellate: CIC (Centre) / SIC (State)
RTI fee: ₹10 (BPL exempt)
DPDPA assent: 11 August 2023
Data Principal: The individual whose data is processed
Data Fiduciary: Who decides purposes & means
Max penalty (DPDPA): ₹250 crore per breach
Regulator (DPDPA): Data Protection Board of India

Chapter 32 — Emergency Provisions — Articles 352, 356 & 360
Part XVIII of the Constitution (Articles 352-360) contains three distinct emergency provisions. Each has
different grounds, approval requirements, duration, and effects. These are heavily tested in APFC
exams because emergencies directly affect labour rights (strikes may be banned, ordinances may
override labour laws). The 44th Amendment 1978 tightened National Emergency — changing 'internal
disturbance' to 'armed rebellion' and requiring Cabinet's written advice.

⚠ EXAM ALERT
44th Amendment 1978 changes to Art 352: (1) 'Internal disturbance' replaced by 'armed rebellion' —
harder to invoke. (2) President can only proclaim on written advice of the Cabinet (Union Cabinet, not just
PM). (3) Lok Sabha can revoke proclamation by simple majority. (4) Revocation resolution: if 1/10 of Lok Sabha
members give notice, a special sitting must be held. These changes were a direct response to the abuse during
the 1975-77 Emergency.

QUICK RECALL
National Emergency: Art 352 — external aggression / armed rebellion
State Emergency (PR): Art 356 — constitutional machinery failed
Financial Emergency: Art 360 — financial stability threatened
All three in Part: XVIII of the Constitution
NE approval: Special Majority (2/3 + majority of total) within 1 month
PR / FE approval: Simple Majority within 2 months

NE revoke: LS simple majority resolution or President
44th Amdt change: 'Armed rebellion' replaced 'internal disturbance'

Chapter 32 — Civil Services under the Constitution — Articles
309-311
Articles 309-311 are critical for APFC because APFCs are civil servants under the Union. Article 309
gives the legislative power to regulate service conditions; Article 310 establishes the 'doctrine of pleasure'
(the default tenure rule); Article 311 provides the crucial procedural safeguards (inquiry before dismissal).
UPSC EPFO questions frequently test which article does what and the exceptions to Art 311.

32B.1 Articles at a Glance
Article

Provision

Key Exam Point

309

Parliament (for Union services) and State Legislature (for State
services) may regulate recruitment and conditions of
service of persons appointed to public services. Until such law,
President / Governor may make rules.

DOPT, CCS (CCA) Rules 1965 flow from
Art 309; EPFO service conditions framed
under Art 309

310

Doctrine of Pleasure: Every civil servant holds office during
the pleasure of the President (Union) or Governor (State). No
fixed tenure guaranteed by default — can be terminated at
pleasure.

Exception: members of All-India
Services, judges, CAG, CEC have
constitutional protection; contract
appointments can have fixed terms

311(1)

A civil servant shall not be dismissed or removed by an
authority subordinate to the appointing authority.

Dismissal must be by appointing
authority or higher, never lower

311(2)

No dismissal / removal / reduction in rank without: (a)
reasonable opportunity to be heard at the inquiry; (b)
opportunity to show cause against the penalty proposed. The
right is procedural — principle of natural justice (audi alteram
partem).

Even voluntary resignation doesn't
attract Art 311; reduction in rank IS
covered (dismissal, removal, reduction
are the 3 penalties)

311(2) Ex
ceptions
—3
clauses

Inquiry not required if: (a) Security of the State — Govt
reasonably concludes inquiry not reasonably practicable; (b) civil
servant convicted by criminal court; (c) Govt satisfied inquiry
is not reasonably practicable or it is not expedient in the
interests of State security.

Security of State is the broadest
exception — any of 3 exceptions
removes the inquiry requirement
entirely

'Show
cause'
(second
stage)

After inquiry, employee must be given a copy of inquiry report
and opportunity to show cause before the penalty is imposed
(post Tulsi Ram Patel SC 1985 — this second opportunity was
curtailed; major amendment to CCS rules followed).

Post Tulsi Ram Patel: 'show cause
against penalty' stage now only required
in major penalties; minor penalties
simplified

✔ KEY FACT
APFC & Art 311: APFCs are Group A Gazetted officers appointed by Union Public Service Commission / Central
Government. If EPFO proposes to dismiss an APFC, the appointing authority (Central Govt) must conduct the
inquiry — a Regional PF Commissioner cannot dismiss an APFC. This is Art 311(1) protection in practice. The
inquiry follows CCS (CCA) Rules 1965 framed under Art 309.

QUICK RECALL
Art 309: Legislature regulates recruitment & service conditions
Art 310: Doctrine of Pleasure — tenure at President's / Governor's pleasure
Art 311(1): Dismissal only by appointing authority or higher
Art 311(2): Inquiry + show cause before dismissal / removal / reduction in rank
3 Exceptions to 311(2): Security of state; criminal conviction; impracticable or not expedient
3 covered penalties: Dismissal · Removal · Reduction in rank
Not covered by 311: Minor penalties (censure, fine, withholding increment)

CCS Rules: Framed under Art 309 — governs EPFO officer discipline

Chapter 32 — Polity PYQ Drill — EO/AO 2025 Trap Questions
A. RTI Act — Appointment Committee is PM, NOT CJI; NO Reappointment for anyone
⚠ EXAM ALERT
RTI Act 2005 — TWO common wrong answers:
Trap 1 — Who heads the appointment committee?
The committee that recommends CIC and Information Commissioners is headed by the Prime Minister
(Chairperson), with the Leader of Opposition in Lok Sabha and a Union Cabinet Minister nominated by the PM.
WRONG answer: Chief Justice of India — CJI has NO role in RTI appointments.
Trap 2 — Reappointment eligibility:
NEITHER the Chief Information Commissioner NOR any Information Commissioner is eligible for reappointment
after completing their term. EO/AO 2025 Q117 tested both traps → Answer D = Neither 1 nor 2 (both statements
wrong).
RTI Provision

Correct Detail

Appointment committee chair

Prime Minister (not CJI, not Speaker, not President)

Term of CIC & ICs

3 years (post RTI Amendment Act 2019; earlier was 5 years)

Age cap

65 years (whichever earlier — term or age)

Reappointment

NOT eligible — neither CIC nor any IC after their term

Salary

CIC = Chief Election Commissioner salary; IC = Election Commissioner salary
(before 2019 Amendment); now Central Govt decides

by Finance Ministers

with Legislature

not a member

✔ KEY FACT
GST Council (Article 279A): The Union Finance Minister chairs it. PM is NOT a member — this is a recurring
exam question. EO/AO 2025 Q116 + APFC 2023 both tested this → Answer A (PM is NOT a member).

D. Advisory Jurisdiction — ONLY Supreme Court (NOT High Courts)
Jurisdiction Type

Who Has It

Who Does NOT Have It

Advisory Jurisdiction
(Art 143)

ONLY the Supreme Court — President can
refer questions of law/fact to SC for an opinion

High Courts do NOT have advisory
jurisdiction

Contempt jurisdiction

BOTH Supreme Court (Art 129) AND High Courts
(Art 215) can punish for contempt of themselves

N/A — both have this

Original jurisdiction

SC has exclusive original jurisdiction in
inter-state disputes (Art 131)

High Courts do NOT have exclusive
inter-state dispute jurisdiction

Writ jurisdiction

SC (Art 32 — only FRs) + High Courts (Art 226 —
FRs + other legal rights)

District Courts do NOT have writ
jurisdiction

✔ KEY FACT
EO/AO 2025 Q119: 'Both SC and HCs have advisory jurisdiction' = FALSE. ONLY SC under Art 143. Also: 'Only SC
can punish for contempt of itself' = FALSE because HCs also have contempt jurisdiction. → Answer D = Neither 1
nor 2.

F. Supreme Court — Retired SC Judges Cannot Practice IN the SC
⚠ EXAM ALERT

EPFO APFC Complete Study Notes · Page 127 · Verified April 2026

Source

Article 124(7) — Retired SC Judge practice restriction:
A person who has been a Judge of the Supreme Court shall NOT plead or act in any court or before any authority
within the territory of India... except in the Supreme Court itself.
Wait — re-read: 'shall not plead or act in any court or authority within India' EXCEPT the Supreme Court. So
retired SC judges CAN appear in SC? No — this is the opposite trap. The actual wording of Art 124(7): retired
judges are barred from appearing in courts other than the SC.
EO/AO 2025 Q109 statement 3: 'A retired SC judge can practice ONLY in SC and not in any other court' — this
was listed as one of the statements to evaluate. The correct position is nuanced — Constitution bars appearance
in OTHER courts, implying SC is the only permissible forum. The answer key treated statements 2 and 4 as
correct (Answer D).

Accreditation

'A' status = fully compliant with Paris Principles; 'B' status = partially compliant

NHRC India

Accredited 'A' status (per EO/AO 2025 Q114 — all 3 statements treated as correct → Answer C)

Significance

GANHRI 'A' status allows NHRC to participate in UN Human Rights Council sessions and speak
independently

Chapter 32B — DPSP Articles + Cross-Paper Polity Precision
Directive Principles articles are tested with specific wage/right type. Know the EXACT article → exact
concept for each. Cross-paper polity traps from 2020–2023 analysis follow.

A. DPSP Labour-Related Articles (Art 37–43A)
Article

Directive Principle

Exact Type / Trap

Art 37

DPSPs are not justiciable (not enforceable by courts)

Trap: say 'not binding on State' — wrong; Art
37 says they are FUNDAMENTAL in governance
even if non-justiciable.

Art 39

Equal pay for equal work for men and women

Tested in polity papers. Art 39(d) = equal pay
for equal work. Do NOT mix with Art 43 (living
wage).

Art 41

Right to work, education, and public assistance in cases
of unemployment, old age, sickness, disablement

Contains the right to WORK — but it's a DPSP,
not FR. MGNREGA operationalises this.

Art 42

Provision for just and humane conditions of work
and maternity relief

Maternity Benefit Act 1961 gives effect to Art
42. Different from Art 43 (living wage).

Art 43

Living Wage + decent standard of life for workers (=
LIVING WAGE, not minimum wage)

Tested 2023 APFC (Q41). Answer = Living
Wage. Trap: 'minimum wage' or 'fair wage'. Art
43 = Living Wage only.

Art 43A

Workers' participation in management of industries

Added by 42nd Amendment 1976. Distinct
from Art 43. Operationalised through Works
Committees (IDA Sec 3).

Art 43B

Promotion of co-operative societies

Added by 97th Amendment 2011. Part IX-B
also added. Both Art 43B and Part IX-B relate
to co-ops.

⚠ EXAM ALERT
DPSP Wage Hierarchy (exam trap):
Minimum Wage = legal floor (Minimum Wages Act 1948 → Code on Wages 2019).
Fair Wage = above minimum, linked to industry capacity.
Living Wage = Article 43 DPSP aspiration — highest level, enables decent living.
Art 39 = equal pay | Art 42 = humane conditions + maternity | Art 43 = Living Wage | Art 43A = workers in
management.

Topic

Correct Fact

Trap / Source

Governor's Ordinance
Power

advice. Covers State List + Concurrent List
only (NOT Union List).

'Governor has discretion' = WRONG.
Trap: 'All three Lists' = WRONG — only
State + Concurrent.

also charged.

Art 43 wage type: LIVING Wage (not minimum, not fair)
Art 39(d): Equal pay for equal work — men and women
Art 42: Humane conditions of work + maternity relief

Art 43B: Co-operative societies (97th Amdt 2011)

MPC elected members: At least 2/3rd elected

Chapter 32 — Constitutional Officers & Finance Commission —
Critical Gaps
Coverage gap closure. These topics are tested every paper but were underrepresented in earlier
chapters: Attorney General (Art 76), Solicitor General, Advocate General (Art 165), and the Finance
Commission (Art 280). Memorise the appointing authority, qualification, and tenure for each — these are
the angle most APFC questions take.

32C.1 Attorney General of India (Article 76)
Aspect

Provision

Constitutional basis

Article 76 — Attorney General for India

Appointed by

President of India (on advice of Council of Ministers)

Qualification

Must be qualified to be appointed as a Judge of the Supreme Court (i.e., 5 yrs HC judge
OR 10 yrs HC advocate OR distinguished jurist)

Tenure

NOT fixed by Constitution — holds office during the pleasure of the President

Removal

May be removed by President at any time (no specific procedure)

Resignation

By submitting resignation to President (convention: when Govt changes)

Salary & allowances

Determined by President (NOT charged on Consolidated Fund of India unlike SC judges)

Functions

(1) Give legal advice to Govt of India on matters referred to him · (2) Appear in cases
involving Govt of India in SC + HCs · (3) Discharge other duties of legal character
assigned by President · (4) Right of audience in all courts in India

Right to vote

Has right to speak and participate in Parliament & Committees · Does NOT have right
to vote · NOT a member of either House

Restrictions

(1) Cannot advise/defend against Govt of India · (2) Cannot defend accused in criminal
cases without Govt sanction · (3) Cannot hold brief without permission of Govt

Position

NOT a member of Cabinet; NOT a Government servant; Highest law officer of India

32C.2 Solicitor General + Additional Solicitors General
Aspect

Solicitor General

Additional SGs

Constitutional basis

NOT a constitutional post — statutory (Law
Officers Rules)

Same — statutory

Appointed by

Appointed by Govt of India (Appointments
Committee of Cabinet)

Govt of India

Role

Assists AG · Appears in cases referred by Govt ·
Second-highest law officer

Assist SG & AG in specific matters

Tenure

Usually 3 years (renewable)

3 years (renewable)

32C.3 Advocate General of the State (Article 165)
Aspect

Provision

Constitutional basis

Article 165 — Advocate General for the State

Appointed by

Governor of the State

Qualification

Qualified to be a Judge of High Court

Tenure

NOT fixed — holds office during pleasure of Governor

Functions

State's equivalent of AG — legal advice + appearance in cases · Right to audience in all state
courts · Right to speak in State Legislature (no vote)

Remuneration

Determined by Governor

EPFO APFC Complete Study Notes · Page 131 · Verified April 2026

✔ KEY FACT
AG vs Solicitor General vs Advocate General — Memory Hook:
AG (Centre) → Article 76 → appointed by President → SC-judge qualification → CONSTITUTIONAL post
SG (Centre) → NO constitutional basis → statutory → assists AG → 3-yr term
AdvG (State) → Article 165 → appointed by Governor → HC-judge qualification → CONSTITUTIONAL post
Trap: 'AG votes in Parliament?' → NO. Speaks/participates only. 'AG is a member of Cabinet?' → NO. 'AG
defends against Govt of India?' → NO — explicit restriction.

32C.4 Finance Commission (Article 280) — Critical for Polity + Economy
Aspect

Provision

Constitutional basis

Article 280 — Finance Commission

Constituted by

President of India

Frequency

Every 5 years OR earlier as President considers necessary

Composition

1 Chairperson + 4 Members (5 total)

Qualifications (S.5 of
FC Act)

Chairperson: person with public-affairs experience
Members: HC judges OR finance/economics/accounts experts OR administrators

Tenure

Specified by President in the constituting order

Mandate (Art 280(3))

(a) Distribution of net tax proceeds between Centre & States (vertical) · (b) Allocation among
States (horizontal) · (c) Grants-in-aid to States (Art 275) · (d) Measures to augment
Consolidated Fund of States · (e) Any other matter referred by President

Nature of
recommendations

Advisory — NOT binding on Govt; but conventionally accepted in full

Reports presented to

President → laid before Parliament with Action Taken Report

⚠ EXAM ALERT
FC Quick Traps:
(1) Q: 'FC is constitutional or statutory?' → Constitutional (Art 280). (2) Q: 'Are FC recommendations binding?'
→ NO, advisory. (3) Q: 'How many FCs since 1950?' → 16 (16th FC is current). (4) Q: 'Who appoints FC?' →
President, NOT Parliament. (5) Q: 'NITI Aayog replaced FC?' → NO. NITI replaced Planning Commission. FC
continues separately. (6) Q: 'GST Council vs FC?' → GST Council = Art 279A (tax administration); FC = Art 280
(tax distribution). Both are constitutional bodies.

Chapter 32 — Constitutional Amendments — High-Hit Drill
1-2 marks every paper. Don't try to memorise all 105 amendments — focus on the ~15 LANDMARK
amendments that are repeatedly tested. For each: year + headline change. Group by theme so they
stick.
✔ KEY FACT
STUDY FILTER — Constitutional Amendments | Tier 1 (1-2 marks)
MEMORIZE — these 12 by year + headline: 1st (1951)=9th Schedule · 42nd (1976)=Mini Constitution;
FDs added; 'Socialist Secular Integrity' in Preamble · 44th (1978)=Right to Property REMOVED from FRs (now
Art 300A) · 52nd (1985)=Anti-Defection (10th Schedule) · 61st (1988)=Voting age 21→18 · 73rd+74th
(1992)=Panchayati Raj + Municipalities · 86th (2002)=RTE (Art 21A) · 97th (2011)=Cooperatives (Art 43B) ·
101st (2016)=GST (Art 246A, 269A, 279A) · 102nd (2018)=NCBC constitutional · 103rd (2019)=EWS 10% ·
105th (2021)=State SEBC power restored
SKIP: Memorising all 105 amendments by number · Specific sub-clauses added/removed unless landmark ·
Pre-1971 amendments beyond 1st and 7th (linguistic states)
TRAP: Right to Property = NOT a FR anymore (44th Am 1978 made it Art 300A — legal right only). FDs were
borrowed from USSR (NOT UK). Don't confuse 102nd (NCBC Art 338B) with 103rd (EWS) — both 2018-19 but
different topics. Basic Structure Doctrine = Kesavananda 1973 (NOT an amendment — a SC ruling).

The 4 'GST Cluster' Articles (memorise — frequently tested):
Art 246A — Power to make laws on GST (Centre + States)
Art 269A — Levy & collection of GST on inter-state trade

Art 366(12A) — Definition of GST

3/4th majority · Centre = 1/3 vote weight · States = 2/3 vote weight.

32D.2 Constitutional Amendment Procedure (Art 368) — Common Trap
Type

Requirement

Examples

Simple Majority

Majority of MPs PRESENT & VOTING in each House ·
NOT under Art 368 (technically outside) · No
President assent special note

Admission/establishment of new
states, Schedule 5 & 6 changes,
official language changes

Special Majority (Art
368(2))

(1) Majority of TOTAL MEMBERSHIP of each House
AND
(2) 2/3rd of MEMBERS PRESENT & VOTING
Both conditions must be met

Most amendments (e.g., 42nd,
44th, 73rd, 74th, 86th)

Special Majority + State
Ratification

Special majority + ratification by at least HALF the
states
(Centre-State relations, Fed structure, Schedule 7
changes)

GST (101st), 73rd, 74th, election
of President/VP, extent of
executive power, Supreme Court
& HC powers

⚠ EXAM ALERT
The Basic Structure Doctrine (Kesavananda 1973): Parliament can amend Constitution under Art 368 BUT
cannot alter the basic structure. What is 'basic structure'? — Not exhaustively defined but includes:
Supremacy of Constitution, Rule of Law, Independence of Judiciary, Federalism, Secularism, Separation of
Powers, Free & Fair Elections, Judicial Review (Minerva Mills 1980), Welfare State, etc.
The 42nd Amendment's attempt to make DPSP & Art 31C override FRs was struck down in Minerva Mills
1980 as violating basic structure.

QUICK RECALL
1st Amendment year: 1951 — Added 9th Schedule
42nd Amendment: 1976 (Mini Constitution); FDs added

EPFO APFC Complete Study Notes · Page 136 · Verified April 2026

Fundamental Duties added by: 42nd Amendment (1976)
Right to Property — current: Legal right Art 300A (NOT FR after 44th Am 1978)
Anti-defection law: 52nd Am 1985 (10th Schedule)
Voting age 21→18: 61st Amendment 1988
Panchayati Raj: 73rd Am 1992 (Part IX, 11th Sch)
Municipalities: 74th Am 1992 (Part IXA, 12th Sch)
Right to Education FR: 86th Am 2002 (Art 21A; children 6-14)
Cooperatives in DPSP: 97th Am 2011 (Art 43B)
GST: 101st Am 2016 (Art 246A, 269A, 279A)
NCBC constitutional: 102nd Am 2018 (Art 338B)
EWS 10% Reservation: 103rd Am 2019 (Art 15(6), 16(6))
Anglo-Indian seats abolished: 104th Am 2020
State SEBC power restored: 105th Am 2021
Amendment procedure: Art 368 — Simple / Special / Special+State
State ratification needed: Min half states (Centre-State / Fed matters)
Basic Structure: Kesavananda 1973 (Parliament cannot amend it)

LAST-MINUTE REVISION — Constitutional Amendments
Q1: 42nd Amendment year + key change? → 1976 · 'Mini Constitution' · added FDs · Socialist/Secular in
Preamble
Q2: Right to Property removed by? → 44th Am (1978) · now Art 300A (legal right only)
Q3: Voting age 21→18? → 61st Am (1988)
Q4: Panchayati Raj? → 73rd Am (1992) · Part IX · 11th Schedule (29 subjects)
Q5: Municipalities? → 74th Am (1992) · Part IXA · 12th Schedule (18 subjects)
Q6: RTE (Art 21A)? → 86th Am (2002) · children 6-14
Q7: Cooperatives DPSP? → 97th Am (2011) · Art 43B
Q8: GST? → 101st Am (2016) · Art 246A, 269A, 279A
Q9: NCBC constitutional? → 102nd Am (2018) · Art 338B
Q10: EWS 10% reservation? → 103rd Am (2019) · Art 15(6) + 16(6)
Q11: Anglo-Indian seats abolished? → 104th Am (2020)
Q12: State SEBC power restored? → 105th Am (2021)
Q13: FDs borrowed from? → USSR (NOT UK)

Q15: Amendment procedure? → Art 368 · 3 types: Simple / Special / Special+State Ratification
EXAM-EVE CHECKLIST:
□ 42nd = 'Mini Constitution' (Indira Gandhi era)
□ Right to Property is NOT a FR anymore (post-44th Am)
□ 73rd + 74th came TOGETHER in 1992 (rural + urban local self-govt)
□ Total amendments to date = ~106

PART IX

Accounting · Auditing · Insurance · Statistics
The quantitative spine of the APFC examination

Chapter 33 — Accounting Principles
Accounting records financial transactions using a double-entry system and follows three Golden Rules
(one for each account type). Twelve accounting concepts/conventions guide how these rules are
applied. Every APFC paper has at least 4-5 questions from this chapter.
✔ KEY FACT
STUDY FILTER — Accounting Principles | Tier 1 (3-4 marks)
MEMORIZE: 3 Golden Rules: Personal (Dr receiver, Cr giver) · Real (Dr what comes in, Cr what goes out) ·
Nominal (Dr expenses/losses, Cr incomes/gains) · AS-1 fundamental assumptions (3 only): Going Concern,
Consistency, Accrual (GCA) · Capital vs Revenue distinction (asset creation vs day-to-day) · Concepts:
Business Entity, Money Measurement, Cost, Matching, Conservatism (Prudence), Materiality, Realisation
SKIP: Detailed history of accounting standards · Pre-Ind-AS framework details · Specific Ind AS numbers beyond
AS-1, AS-2, AS-3 · Memorizing all 32 Indian Accounting Standards by number
TRAP: 'Matching' is NOT a fundamental assumption — it's a CONCEPT. Only 3 fundamental assumptions exist
(GCA). 'Conservatism' = 'anticipate no profit, provide for all losses' = basis for 'Cost or NRV lower' (AS-2).

33.1 Golden Rules of Accounting
Account Type

Debit Rule

Credit Rule

Example Entry

✔ KEY FACT
Accrual concept is the foundation of modern financial reporting. Under Ind AS and GAAP, cash-basis
accounting is allowed only for very small entities. EPFO itself uses accrual-basis accounting for its statutory
returns.

⚠ EXAM ALERT
Conservatism vs Consistency — both begin with 'Con' but mean different things. Conservatism = provide for
losses; Consistency = same method over time. Don't swap them.

MNEMONIC
Debit the Receiver, Credit the Giver (Personal) · Debit what Comes In, Credit what Goes Out
(Real) · Debit Expenses, Credit Incomes (Nominal)
QUICK RECALL
Personal A/c: Dr Receiver; Cr Giver
Real A/c: Dr what comes in; Cr what goes out
Nominal A/c: Dr Expense/Loss; Cr Income/Gain
Basic equation: Assets = Liabilities + Capital (Dual Aspect)
Accrual: Record when earned/incurred, not cash flow
Going Concern: Assumes business continues

⚠ EXAM ALERT
LAST-MINUTE REVISION — Accounting Principles
Q1: AS-1 fundamental assumptions? → Going Concern, Consistency, Accrual (GCA)
Q2: Is 'Matching' a fundamental assumption? → NO — it's a CONCEPT (trap)
Q3: 'Cost or NRV lower' (AS-2)? → Convention of Conservatism / Prudence
Q4: Capital A/c balance? → CREDIT (Capital is a liability)
Q5: Principal book of accounts? → Ledger (NOT Journal — Journal is book of original entry)
Q6: Purchase of asset wrongly recorded as expense? → Error of PRINCIPLE
Q7: Provision vs Reserve? → Provision = charge against profit; Reserve = appropriation
Q8: Cinema hall licence fee? → REVENUE expenditure (recurring)
Q9: Loans GIVEN by Govt? → Capital Expenditure
Q10: Interest received on Govt loans? → Revenue Receipt
EXAM-EVE CHECKLIST:
□ 3 Fundamental assumptions = GCA only (Going Concern, Consistency, Accrual)
□ 5 error types: Principle, Omission, Commission, Compensating, Clerical
□ Provision = charge (must be made); Reserve = appropriation (after profit)

EPFO APFC Complete Study Notes · Page 139 · Verified April 2026

Chapter 34 — Financial Statements — Trading, P&L;, Balance
Sheet
The three final accounts of any business are the Trading Account (computing Gross Profit), the Profit &
Loss Account (computing Net Profit), and the Balance Sheet (snapshot of position on a date). Gross
Profit flows from Trading to P&L; Net Profit flows from P&L to Balance Sheet via Capital Account.

34.1 The Transaction-to-Statement Flow
Business Transactions
Invoices, receipts, vouchers

Journal Entries
Date-wise chronological record

Ledger Posting
Account-wise re-organisation

Trial Balance
List of ledger balances

Adjustments (accruals, depreciation)
Period-end entries

Trading + P&L + Balance Sheet
Final statements

Figure 34.1 — From transaction to financial statement

34.2 Trading Account
FORMULA / KEY POINTS
Dr side: Opening Stock + Purchases + Direct Expenses (freight inwards, wages, power)
Cr side: Sales + Closing Stock
Gross Profit = (Sales + Closing Stock) − (Opening Stock + Purchases + Direct Expenses)

34.3 Profit & Loss Account
FORMULA / KEY POINTS
Dr side: Indirect Expenses (salaries, rent, admin, selling, depreciation, interest paid)
Cr side: Gross Profit (brought down) + Other Incomes (commission, discount received)
Net Profit = (GP + Other Incomes) − Indirect Expenses

34.4 Balance Sheet
FORMULA / KEY POINTS
Assets = Liabilities + Capital (always; the accounting identity)
Vertical (Schedule III) format in India:

EPFO APFC Complete Study Notes · Page 140 · Verified April 2026

Equity & Liabilities: Equity + Non-current Liabilities + Current Liabilities
Assets: Non-current Assets + Current Assets

✔ KEY FACT
A Balance Sheet is drawn as at a date (say 31 March). The Trading and P&L are drawn for a period (say year
ended 31 March). This distinction is a common question.

MNEMONIC
Trading → GP · P&L → NP · Balance Sheet → Position
QUICK RECALL
GP formula: Sales + CS − (OS + Purchases + Direct Exp)
NP formula: GP + Other income − Indirect Exp
Balance Sheet: A = L + C; always balances
Trading period: For the year ended 31 March
BS nature: As at 31 March
Trial Balance: Arithmetic check; doesn't guarantee correctness

EPFO APFC Complete Study Notes · Page 141 · Verified April 2026

Chapter 35 — Depreciation — SLM vs WDV
Depreciation is the allocation of a fixed asset's cost over its useful life. Two methods dominate —
Straight Line Method (SLM) and Written Down Value (WDV). SLM gives equal annual charge; WDV
gives a constant-percentage charge on declining book value. Income Tax Act permits only WDV for most
assets; Companies Act permits either.

35.1 Method Comparison
STRAIGHT LINE (SLM)
• Formula: (Cost − Scrap) ÷ Useful Life
• Equal amount charged each year
• Book value hits scrap value at end of life
• Permitted: Companies Act (optional)
• Suitable for: constant-utility assets (buildings)

WRITTEN DOWN VALUE (WDV)
• Formula: Fixed % × Opening Book Value
• High charge early; low later
• Book value approaches zero asymptotically
• Permitted: Income Tax Act (mandatory for most)
• Suitable for: tech/machinery (obsolescence)

35.2 Worked Example — ₹1,00,000 machine
FORMULA / KEY POINTS
Asset: machine costing ₹1,00,000; useful life 5 years; scrap ₹10,000.
SLM: (1,00,000 − 10,000) ÷ 5 = ₹18,000 per year (constant)
Year 1 book value: 82,000 | Year 2: 64,000 | … | Year 5: 10,000
WDV @ 30%:
Year 1: 1,00,000 × 30% = ₹30,000 → BV ₹70,000
Year 2: 70,000 × 30% = ₹21,000 → BV ₹49,000
Year 3: 49,000 × 30% = ₹14,700 → BV ₹34,300
Year 4: 34,300 × 30% = ₹10,290 → BV ₹24,010
Year 5: 24,010 × 30% = ₹7,203 → BV ₹16,807
Under WDV, total depreciation over 5 years = ₹83,193 (vs ₹90,000 SLM).

⚠ EXAM ALERT
Under WDV, the asset never fully depreciates to zero — always a residual. Under SLM, the asset reaches
scrap value exactly at end of useful life. This is a common conceptual trap.

MNEMONIC
SLM = Equal slices · WDV = Front-loaded → IT Act prefers WDV
QUICK RECALL
SLM formula: (Cost − Scrap) ÷ Life
WDV formula: Fixed % × Opening book value
Constant charge: SLM
Decreasing charge: WDV
Income Tax Act: WDV method for most assets

EPFO APFC Complete Study Notes · Page 142 · Verified April 2026

Companies Act: Either method permitted (declare consistently)

EPFO APFC Complete Study Notes · Page 143 · Verified April 2026

Chapter 36 — Ratio Analysis
Ratios summarise financial statements into a handful of interpretable numbers. Five families: Liquidity,
Profitability, Activity, Leverage, Investor ratios.
✔ KEY FACT
STUDY FILTER — Ratio Analysis | Tier 2 (1-2 marks)
MEMORIZE these 8 ratios + benchmarks: Current Ratio = CA / CL (ideal 2:1) · Quick (Acid Test) = (CA −
Inventory) / CL (ideal 1:1) · Debt-Equity = LT Debt / Equity (ideal ≤ 2:1) · Gross Profit % = GP / Sales × 100 ·
Net Profit % = NP / Sales × 100 · ROI = Net Profit / Investment × 100 · EPS = Net Profit available to Equity /
No. of Equity Shares · Stock Turnover = Sales / Avg Inventory
SKIP: Memorizing all 20+ ratios · Industry-specific benchmark ratios · Detailed DuPont 3-factor analysis ·
Specific ROCE vs ROI distinction nuances
TRAP: Current Ratio uses ALL current assets; Quick Ratio EXCLUDES inventory. Don't confuse 'Working Capital'
(CA − CL, an absolute amount) with 'Current Ratio' (CA / CL, a ratio). Operating Profit = NP + Interest + Tax
(specific definition for the Q).

Chapter 37 — Auditing
Auditing is the independent examination of an entity's financial statements and underlying records.
The APFC syllabus covers types of audit, audit techniques, audit opinions, and — crucially — the role of
the Comptroller & Auditor General (CAG) in auditing EPFO.
✔ KEY FACT
STUDY FILTER — Auditing | Tier 2 (1-2 marks)
MEMORIZE — Section #s MATTER here (Companies Act 2013): Sec 139 — Auditor appointment (first 30
days; subsequent 5 yrs) · Sec 143 — Powers & duties · Sec 144 — 9 prohibited services (Internal audit,
Investment advisory, etc.) · Rotation: Individual = 5 yrs max; Firm = 10 yrs max; Cool-off = 5 yrs · Fraud
reporting: ≥ ₹1 crore to Central Govt (Sec 143(12)) · Audit opinions (U/Q/A/D): Unmodified, Qualified,
Adverse, Disclaimer · Vouching (transactions) vs Verification (closing balances)
SKIP: Memorizing every type of audit · Detailed CARO 2020 21-point list · Specific audit programme templates ·
Sampling formula derivations
TRAP: Internal audit (Sec 138) ≠ Statutory audit (Sec 139). Vouching ≠ Verification — vouching is
TRANSACTIONS during the year, verification is BALANCES at year-end. First auditor = 30 days appointment by
BOARD; subsequent = by AGM.

37.4 CAG & EPFO Audit
FORMULA / KEY POINTS
Article 148: Comptroller & Auditor General of India.
Appointment: by President of India.
Tenure: 6 years OR 65 years, whichever earlier.
Removal: same manner as Supreme Court judge.
Salary & allowances: charged on Consolidated Fund of India (not subject to vote).
EPF audit basis: Section 5A(6) of EPF & MP Act, 1952 — CAG audits the accounts of the Central Board of
Trustees.

⚠ EXAM ALERT
Qualified ≠ Adverse. A Qualified opinion means "true and fair, EXCEPT for X"; an Adverse opinion means
"NOT true and fair at all". Huge practical difference — and a favourite MCQ.

Chapter 38 — Insurance Principles
Seven classical principles govern every insurance contract. Only some of them apply to Life Insurance —
most notably, indemnity does NOT. These distinctions are the heart of insurance MCQs in the APFC
exam.
✔ KEY FACT
STUDY FILTER — Insurance Principles + Types + IRDAI | Tier 1 (3-4 marks)
MEMORIZE: 7 Principles (I-U-I-S-C-P-L): Insurable Interest · Utmost Good Faith · Indemnity (does NOT
apply to LIFE) · Subrogation · Contribution · Proximate Cause · Loss Min · Life Insurance Types: Term · Whole
Life · Endowment · ULIP · General Insurance: Fire · Marine (OLDEST) · Motor (3rd party MANDATORY) · Health ·
IRDAI: 1999, HQ Hyderabad, 10 members, FDI 74% (2021) · Life = ASSURANCE (NOT indemnity); General
= INDEMNITY
SKIP: Memorizing every insurance company history · Detailed Insurance Act 1938 section numbers (only Sec 39
= nomination) · Specific policy template wording · Specific rider types beyond critical illness
TRAP: Indemnity does NOT apply to Life Insurance — because human life has no measurable monetary value.
Life = ASSURANCE (paid on certain event). Sum Assured (life) ≠ Sum Insured (general). Free-look period = 15
days (30 if distance marketing).
Principle

Explanation

Wrong application

of insurance

After paying claim, insurer steps

for bigger claim → claim
rejected

38.1 Life vs Non-Life Insurance
LIFE INSURANCE
• Subject: human life (priceless)
• Indemnity principle does NOT apply
• Can over-insure (multiple policies fine)
• Sum assured fixed at inception
• Contract of assurance (paid on certain event)
• Long-term contracts (10/20/30+ years)
• Regulator: IRDAI (Life Insurance vertical)
• Examples: Term plans, ULIPs, endowment

NON-LIFE (GENERAL) INSURANCE
• Subject: property, liability, health
• Indemnity principle APPLIES
• No over-insurance — pays only actual loss
• Sum assured determined by policy + actual loss
• Contract of indemnity (paid only on actual loss)
• Short-term (usually 1 year renewable)
• Regulator: IRDAI (Non-Life vertical)
• Examples: Motor, Fire, Marine, Health, Travel

✔ KEY FACT

EPFO APFC Complete Study Notes · Page 150 · Verified April 2026

Indemnity does NOT apply to Life Insurance because human life has no measurable financial value in the
indemnity sense. Life insurance is a contract of assurance, not indemnity.

⚠ EXAM ALERT
Contribution (multiple insurers share) requires concurrent insurance. Common in Fire & Marine; rare in Life
(usually multiple full-sum policies valid).

MNEMONIC
I-U-I-S-C-P-L → Insurable Interest · Utmost Good Faith · Indemnity · Subrogation · Contribution
· Proximate Cause · Loss Min.
QUICK RECALL
7 principles: I-U-I-S-C-P-L
Life insurance: Indemnity NOT applicable
Regulator: IRDAI (Insurance Regulatory & Dev Authority)
Uberrima Fides: Utmost good faith — full disclosure
Subrogation: Transfer of rights after claim settlement
Proximate Cause: Effective cause, not remote cause

Ch-38 Patch — Types of Insurance + IRDAI (HIGH HIT)
Beyond the 7 principles, APFC papers test types of life and general insurance + IRDAI. These add 2-3
marks reliably. Master the four life-insurance types (Term · Whole Life · Endowment · ULIP) and the four
general-insurance types (Fire · Marine · Motor · Health).
